Like if I ignore it, something bad might happen. It’s clear, like a man right there by me, from the vents or under the hood. It also tells me not to trust my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or people, when no one is around?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see people or shadows like that. It’s more that I hear him — the man’s voice — coming from the vents or under the hood, like he’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe what the voice tells you about your husband?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. But I know sometimes it probably comes out too fast or not as clear to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're having trouble expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. I can hear people telling me to slow down, my husband or coworkers, but in the moment stopping feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are logically connected when you're hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. When it’s happening, it can feel connected to me in the moment, but afterward I don’t always know if it actually made sense or if I was just following it because it was in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when you hear the voice are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like it’s coming from me. It sounds like a man’s voice, like it’s outside of me—sometimes from the garage vents or under the hood. It’s clear, like someone is right there, telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. But I know sometimes it probably comes out too fast or not as clear to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're having trouble expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. I can hear people telling me to slow down, my husband or coworkers, but in the moment stopping feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are logically connected when you're hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. When it’s happening, it can feel connected to me in the moment, but afterward I don’t always know if it actually made sense or if I was just following it because it was in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when you hear the voice are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like it’s coming from me. It sounds like a man’s voice, like it’s outside of me—sometimes from the garage vents or under the hood. It’s clear, like someone is right there, telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you have, aside from the voice, are your own?\n\nPatient: I think so, mostly. I mean, the regular thoughts feel like mine. It’s more the voice that feels separate. The rest is just… my mind racing or worrying, but it still feels like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep hearing this man’s voice… like it’s coming from the garage vents, or sometimes from under the hood when I’m working. It sounds clear, like someone standing right next to me. He tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes tells me not to touch certain cars. It’s been really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes. It tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes it says not to touch certain cars when I’m working. It’s really clear, like the man is right there near me.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something that you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when it tells me not to touch certain cars, it feels like I have to listen. Like if I ignore it, something bad might happen. It’s clear, like a man right there by me, from the vents or under the hood. It also tells me not to trust my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or people, when no one is around?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see people or shadows like that. It’s more that I hear him — the man’s voice — coming from the vents or under the hood, like he’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe what the voice tells you about your husband?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. I can hear people telling me to slow down, my husband or coworkers, but in the moment stopping feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are logically connected when you're hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. When it’s happening, it can feel connected to me in the moment, but afterward I don’t always know if it actually made sense or if I was just following it because it was in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when you hear the voice are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like it’s coming from me. It sounds like a man’s voice, like it’s outside of me—sometimes from the garage vents or under the hood.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep hearing this man’s voice… like it’s coming from the garage vents, or sometimes from under the hood when I’m working. It sounds clear, like someone standing right next to me. He tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes tells me not to touch certain cars. It’s been really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes. It tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes it says not to touch certain cars when I’m working. It’s really clear, like the man is right there near me.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something that you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when it tells me not to touch certain cars, it feels like I have to listen. Like if I ignore it, something bad might happen. It’s clear, like a man right there by me, from the vents or under the hood. It also tells me not to trust my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or people, when no one is around?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see people or shadows like that. It’s more that I hear him — the man’s voice — coming from the vents or under the hood, like he’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe what the voice tells you about your husband?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. But I know sometimes it probably comes out too fast or not as clear to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're having trouble expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. I can hear people telling me to slow down, my husband or coworkers, but in the moment stopping feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are logically connected when you're hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. When it’s happening, it can feel connected to me in the moment, but afterward I don’t always know if it actually made sense or if I was just following it because it was in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when you hear the voice are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like it’s coming from me. It sounds like a man’s voice, like it’s outside of me—sometimes from the garage vents or under the hood. It’s clear, like someone is right there, telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you have, aside from the voice, are your own?\n\nPatient: I think so, mostly. I mean, the regular thoughts feel like mine. It’s more the voice that feels separate. The rest is just… my mind racing or worrying, but it still feels like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice and the thoughts it inspires are affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been affecting things a lot. At work I’ve made mistakes at the shop, and they pulled me off the high-voltage EV jobs because people were worried I wasn’t being safe. And at home… the arguing with my husband got bad enough that he took me in for an emergency evaluation. So yeah, it’s not just in my head, it’s messing up my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fast speech is related to the voice you hear or is it something that happens independently of the voice?\n\nPatient: I think it happens on its own, not just because of the voice. Once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, I just talk really fast and the words keep coming. My husband and coworkers tell me to slow down, but it feels almost impossible to stop once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t always notice them being jumbled when the voice isn’t there. Sometimes I’m just thinking fast, but I can usually follow what I mean, even if other people might have trouble keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice influences your thoughts even when you're not directly hearing it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even when I’m not hearing it right that second, it kind of sticks with me. Like if that man’s voice was saying not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars, I keep thinking about it after. It came from the garage vents or under the hood so clearly, like someone was right there, and then it’s hard to just shake it off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. It’s been really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes. It tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes it says not to touch certain cars when I’m working. It’s really clear, like the man is right there near me.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something that you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when it tells me not to touch certain cars, it feels like I have to listen. Like if I ignore it, something bad might happen. It’s clear, like a man right there by me, from the vents or under the hood. It also tells me not to trust my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or people, when no one is around?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see people or shadows like that. It’s more that I hear him — the man’s voice — coming from the vents or under the hood, like he’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe what the voice tells you about your husband?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. But I know sometimes it probably comes out too fast or not as clear to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're having trouble expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. Once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, I just talk really fast and the words keep coming. My husband and coworkers tell me to slow down, but it feels almost impossible to stop once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No… not really. When it’s happening, it feels like it’s his voice telling me things, not just my own thoughts. Like it’s coming from the garage vents or under the hood, clear, like someone standing right there telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars. Then after that I start thinking about what he said, but the voice itself doesn’t feel like me.

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n20\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I keep hearing this man’s voice… like it’s coming from the garage vents, or sometimes from under the hood when I’m working. It sounds clear, like someone standing right next to me. He tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes tells me not to touch certain cars. It’s been really hard to ignore.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something specific?\n\nPatient: Yes. It tells me not to trust my husband, and sometimes it says not to touch certain cars when I’m working. It’s really clear, like the man is right there near me.\n\nDoctor: Does the voice ever tell you to do something that you feel you must do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… when it tells me not to touch certain cars, it feels like I have to listen. Like if I ignore it, something bad might happen. It’s clear, like a man right there by me, from the vents or under the hood. It also tells me not to trust my husband.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever see things that aren't there, like shadows or people, when no one is around?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t really see people or shadows like that. It’s more that I hear him — the man’s voice — coming from the vents or under the hood, like he’s right next to me.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe what the voice tells you about your husband?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I know it probably sounds far-fetched, but I’m still convinced he’s involved somehow. Like him and the shop owner are using the EV chargers and the security cameras to track what I’m doing. Even when they show me the footage and say there’s nothing there, I don’t really believe them.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to keep track of what you're saying when we talk?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I don’t really notice that. Sometimes I might lose my place if I’m stressed, but I think I’m following you okay right now.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking to people?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess I do. Once I start explaining what’s going on or what I’m planning, it just kind of pours out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, but when I’m in it, it feels almost impossible to stop or pause.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are disconnected from each other when you're talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… disconnected isn’t really how I’d put it. My thoughts can feel fast, and I might get ahead of myself, but to me they still feel connected. Other people might not always follow, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your thoughts are organized when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. In my head it usually feels organized enough, like I know what I’m trying to say. But I know sometimes it probably comes out too fast or not as clear to other people.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel like you're having trouble expressing your thoughts in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do. I feel like I know what I mean, but once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, the words come out really fast. My husband and people at work tell me to slow down, and I try, but in the moment it feels almost impossible to stop.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you can control your thoughts when you're hearing the voice or when you're talking to someone?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Once I start talking—like explaining my plans or what’s been happening—it just kind of pours out really fast. I can hear people telling me to slow down, my husband or coworkers, but in the moment stopping feels almost impossible.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are logically connected when you're hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. When it’s happening, it can feel connected to me in the moment, but afterward I don’t always know if it actually made sense or if I was just following it because it was in my head.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when you hear the voice are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No, it doesn’t feel like it’s coming from me. It sounds like a man’s voice, like it’s outside of me—sometimes from the garage vents or under the hood. It’s clear, like someone is right there, telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you have, aside from the voice, are your own?\n\nPatient: I think so, mostly. I mean, the regular thoughts feel like mine. It’s more the voice that feels separate. The rest is just… my mind racing or worrying, but it still feels like me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the voice and the thoughts it inspires are affecting your daily life or relationships in a significant way?\n\nPatient: Yes. It’s been affecting things a lot. At work I’ve made mistakes at the shop, and they pulled me off the high-voltage EV jobs because people were worried I wasn’t being safe. And at home… the arguing with my husband got bad enough that he took me in for an emergency evaluation. So yeah, it’s not just in my head, it’s messing up my life.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fast speech is related to the voice you hear or is it something that happens independently of the voice?\n\nPatient: I think it happens on its own, not just because of the voice. Once I start explaining my plans or what’s been going on, I just talk really fast and the words keep coming. My husband and coworkers tell me to slow down, but it feels almost impossible to stop once I get going.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your thoughts are jumbled or hard to follow when you're not hearing the voice?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t always notice them being jumbled when the voice isn’t there. Sometimes I’m just thinking fast, but I can usually follow what I mean, even if other people might have trouble keeping up.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the voice influences your thoughts even when you're not directly hearing it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Even when I’m not hearing it right that second, it kind of sticks with me. Like if that man’s voice was saying not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars, I keep thinking about it after. It came from the garage vents or under the hood so clearly, like someone was right there, and then it’s hard to just shake it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble making sense when you're talking about something that has nothing to do with the voice?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d say often. I mean, sometimes people look confused when I’m talking, but I usually feel like I know what I’m trying to say. It’s hard for me to tell if I’m not making sense or if I’m just going too fast.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like the thoughts you're having when the voice is telling you something are your own thoughts?\n\nPatient: No… not really. When it’s happening, it feels like it’s his voice telling me things, not just my own thoughts. Like it’s coming from the garage vents or under the hood, clear, like someone standing right there telling me not to trust my husband or not to touch certain cars. Then after that I start thinking about what he said, but the voice itself doesn’t feel like me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
